| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Steven_Spielberg_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Steven_Spielberg_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Steven_Spielberg_sidebar) |

**Steven Allan Spielberg** ([/ˈspiːlbɜːrɡ/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English); born December 18, 1946) is an American filmmaker. A major figure of the [New Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hollywood) era and pioneer of the modern [blockbuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockbuster_\(entertainment\)), Spielberg is widely regarded as one of the greatest and most influential filmmakers in the [history of cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_cinema) and is the [highest-grossing film director of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_film_directors), with [his films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Spielberg_filmography) having earned almost \$11 billion worldwide.[^cite_note-1] Among other [accolades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_nominations_received_by_Steven_Spielberg), he has received three [Academy Awards](324.md), four [Golden Globe Awards](142724.md), four [BAFTA Awards](150200.md), twelve [Emmy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Awards), a [Tony Award](54741.md), and a [Grammy Award](44635.md), as well as the [AFI Life Achievement Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFI_Life_Achievement_Award) in 1995, an [honorary knighthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorary_knighthood) in 2001, the [Kennedy Center Honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Center_Honor) in 2006, the [Cecil B. DeMille Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecil_B._DeMille_Award) in 2009, the [Presidential Medal of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom) in 2015, and the [National Medal of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Medal_of_Arts) in 2023. According to *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)*, he is one of the world's wealthiest celebrities, with a net worth of at least \$5.3 billion.[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-Spielberg-3] He is one of 22 people to achieve [EGOT](361388.md) status.[^cite_note-Davis-4][^cite_note-5]

Spielberg was born in [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati), Ohio. Growing up in [Haddonfield, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haddonfield,_New_Jersey) and [Phoenix, Arizona](49121.md),[^cite_note-\:0-6] he moved to [California](5407.md) and studied film in college. Starting his career in television, Spielberg made his directorial debut with [*Duel*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duel_\(1971_film\)) (1971). His theatrical debut was *[The Sugarland Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sugarland_Express)* (1974), which began his decades-long collaboration with composer [John Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Williams). He became a household name with [*Jaws*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaws_\(film\)) (1975) and earned further critical and commercial success with *[Close Encounters of the Third Kind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Close_Encounters_of_the_Third_Kind)* (1977), *[E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.T._the_Extra-Terrestrial)* (1982) and the original *[Indiana Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones)* trilogy (1981–1989). He explored drama in [*The Color Purple*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Color_Purple_\(1985_film\)) (1985) and [*Empire of the Sun*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_the_Sun_\(film\)) (1987).

Spielberg directed the historical war epics *[Schindler's List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schindler's_List)* (1993)—which has often been listed as one of the [greatest films ever made](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_films_ever_made)—and *[Saving Private Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saving_Private_Ryan)* (1998), both of which won him the [Academy Award for Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director) with the former also earning him [Best Picture](61702.md). He has also directed the science fiction films *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park)* (1993)—which became the [highest-grossing film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highest-grossing_film) at the time[^cite_note-Rubin2022-7]—*[A.I. Artificial Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.I._Artificial_Intelligence)* (2001), *[Minority Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_Report)* (2002), *[War of the Worlds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Worlds_\(2005_film\))* (2005), *[Ready Player One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ready_Player_One_\(film\))* (2018) and *[Disclosure Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disclosure_Day)* (2026); the historical dramas *[Amistad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amistad_\(film\))* (1997), *[Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_\(2005_film\))* (2005), *[War Horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Horse_\(film\))* (2011), *[Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_\(film\))* (2012), *[Bridge of Spies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridge_of_Spies_\(film\))* (2015) and *[The Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Post_\(film\))* (2017); the comedies *[Catch Me If You Can](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch_Me_If_You_Can)* (2002) and *[The Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Terminal)* (2004); the animated film *[The Adventures of Tintin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Tintin_\(film\))* (2011); the musical *[West Side Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Side_Story_\(2021_film\))* (2021); and the family drama *[The Fabelmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fabelmans)* (2022).

Spielberg co-founded [Amblin Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amblin_Entertainment) and [DreamWorks Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DreamWorks_Pictures), and he has served as a producer for many successful films and television series, among them *[Poltergeist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poltergeist_\(1982_film\))* (1982), *[Gremlins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gremlins)* (1984), *[Back to the Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Back_to_the_Future)* (1985), *[An American Tail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_American_Tail)* (1986), *[Who Framed Roger Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_Framed_Roger_Rabbit)* (1988), *[Animaniacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animaniacs)* (1993), *[Pinky and the Brain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinky_and_the_Brain)* (1995), *[Twister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twister_\(1996_film\))* (1996), *[Band of Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band_of_Brothers_\(miniseries\))* (2001) and *[Transformers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transformers_\(film\))* (2007). Several of Spielberg's works are considered among the greatest films in history, and some are among the highest-grossing ever.[^cite_note-8] Seven of his films have been inducted into the [National Film Registry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Film_Registry) by the [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress) as being "culturally, historically or aesthetically significant".[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-\:1-10] In 2013, *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* listed him as one of the [100 most influential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100) people,[^cite_note-11] and in 2023, Spielberg was the recipient of the first ever [*Time* 100 Impact Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time100_Impact_Awards) in the US.[^cite_note-12]

## Early life and education

&#x20;Steven Allan Spielberg was born on December 18, 1946, in Cincinnati, Ohio, the eldest child and only son of four children.[^cite_note-Thomson-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199735-14] His mother, Leah Adler ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names) Posner),[^cite_note-15] was a concert pianist who ran a [kosher dairy restaurant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosher_dairy_restaurant),[^cite_note-16] and his father, [Arnold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Spielberg),[^cite_note-17] was an electrical engineer involved in the development of computers. His immediate family were [Reform Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Jewish)/[Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Jewish).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish20046-18][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxter199638-19][^cite_note-FreshAir-20] Spielberg's paternal grandparents were [Ukrainian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Ukraine);[^cite_note-JJ-21][^cite_note-22] his grandmother Rebecca (née Chechi), was from [Sudylkiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudylkiv), and his grandfather Shmuel Spielberg was from [Kamianets-Podilskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamianets-Podilskyi).[^cite_note-JJ-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199722-23] Spielberg has three younger sisters: [Anne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Spielberg), Sue, and [Nancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Spielberg).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxter199616-24] At their home in Cincinnati, his grandmother taught English to Holocaust survivors. They, in turn, taught him numbers:&#x20;

> One man in particular, I kept looking at his numbers – his number tattooed on his forearm ... he started – you know, when – during the dinner break, when everybody was eating and not learning, he would point to the numbers. And he would say, that is a two, and that is a four. And then he'd say, and this is a eight, and that's a one. And I'll never forget this. And he said, and that's a nine. And then he crooked his arm and inverted his arm and said, and see, it becomes a six. It's magic. And now it's a nine, and now it's a six, and now it's a nine and now it's a six. And that's really how I learned my numbers for the first time ... the irony of all that, and the gift of that lesson, never really dawned on me until I was much older.[^cite_note-FreshAir-20]

In 1952, his family moved to [Haddon Township, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haddon_Township,_New_Jersey), after his father was hired by [RCA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RCA_Corporation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199748-25] Spielberg attended Hebrew school from 1953 to 1957, in classes taught by Rabbi [Albert L. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_L._Lewis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199753-26] In early 1957, the family moved to [Phoenix, Arizona](49121.md).[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish20049-28] Spielberg had a [bar mitzvah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_mitzvah) ceremony when he was thirteen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199736-29] His family was involved in the synagogue and had many Jewish friends.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPogrebin200530-30] Of [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), he said that his parents "talked about it all the time, and so it was always on my mind".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPogrebin200530-30] His father had lost between sixteen and twenty relatives in the Holocaust.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199722-23] Spielberg found it difficult accepting his heritage; he said: "It isn't something I enjoy admitting ... but when I was seven, eight, nine years old, God forgive me, I was embarrassed because we were Orthodox Jews. I was embarrassed by the outward perception of my parents' Jewish practices. I was never really ashamed to be Jewish, but I was uneasy at times."[^cite_note-\:3-31][^cite_note-32] Spielberg was the target of [antisemitism](1078.md): "In high school, I got smacked and kicked around. Two bloody noses. It was horrible."[^cite_note-\:3-31][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199722-23] He gradually followed Judaism less during adolescence, after his family had moved to various neighborhoods and found themselves to be the only Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPogrebin200529-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199720-35]

&#x20;Spielberg recalls his parents taking him to see [Cecil B. DeMille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecil_B._DeMille)'s [*The Greatest Show on Earth*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Greatest_Show_on_Earth_\(film\)) (1952). He had never seen a movie before, and thought they were taking him to the circus. He was terrified by the movie's train crash, and at age 12, recreated it with his [Lionel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lionel_Corporation) trains and filmed it. He recalls: "The trains went around and around, and after a while that got boring, and I had this eight-millimeter camera, and I staged a train wreck and filmed it. That was hard on the trains, but then I could cut the film lots of different ways and look at it over and over again." This was his first home movie.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200411-36][^cite_note-Ebert-37] In 1958, he became a [Boy Scout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scouts_BSA), attaining the rank of [Eagle Scout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle_Scout) at age 13.[^cite_note-Magic-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199778-39] Spielberg used his father's movie camera to make amateur features, and took it along on every Scout trip. He fulfilled a requirement for the photography [merit badge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_badge) by making a nine-minute [8 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_mm_video_format) [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_film), *The Last Gunfight*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199777-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200412-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199783-42] At age 13, Spielberg made a 40-minute [war film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_film), *Escape to Nowhere*, with a cast of classmates. It won first prize in a statewide competition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200416-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199799-44] Spielberg made about fifteen to twenty 8 mm adventure films.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson20075-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199784-46] He recalls that&#x20;

> my dad told me stories about World War II constantly ... I knew, based on the stories my dad and his friends were telling about World War II, that there was no glory in war. And it was ugly, and it was cruel ... it was, you know, visually devastating. And so I thought, someday, if I ever do make a war movie for real, it's got to be something that tells the truth about what those experiences had been for those young 17-, 18-, 19-year-old boys storming Omaha Beach, let's say.[^cite_note-FreshAir-20]

Spielberg went to the local theater every Saturday.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199781-47] Formative films included [Victor Fleming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Fleming)'s *[Captains Courageous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captains_Courageous_\(1937_film\))* (1937), [Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney)'s [*Pinocchio*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinocchio_\(1940_film\)) and [*Fantasia*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fantasia_\(1940_film\)) (both 1940), [Akira Kurosawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Kurosawa)'s *[Rashomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashomon)* (1950) and *[The Seven Samurai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seven_Samurai)* (1954),[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-Prince132-49] [Ishirō Honda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishir%C5%8D_Honda)'s *[Godzilla, King of the Monsters!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godzilla,_King_of_the_Monsters!)* (1956),[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] [David Lean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lean)'s *[Bridge on the River Kwai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridge_on_the_River_Kwai)* (1957) and *[Lawrence of Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_of_Arabia_\(film\))* (1962) ("the film that set me on my journey"), [Alfred Hitchcock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Hitchcock)'s *[The Birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Birds_\(film\))* (1963) and [Stanley Kubrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Kubrick)'s *[Dr. Strangelove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Strangelove)* (1964) and *[2001: A Space Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001:_A_Space_Odyssey)* (1968) ("I'm still living off the adrenalin that ... I experienced watching that film for the first time.")[^cite_note-\:BFI-52] He attended [Arcadia High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcadia_High_School_\(Phoenix,_Arizona\)) for three years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199794-53] He wrote and directed a science fiction film, *[Firelight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firelight_\(1964_film\))*, funded mainly by his father. It was shown in a local theater for one evening and grossed \$501 against its \$500 budget.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997102-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200418-55][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200420-56]

After taking a tour bus to [Universal Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Studios,_Inc.), a chance conversation with an executive led to Spielberg getting a three-day pass to the premises. On the fourth day, he walked up to the studio gates without a pass, and the security guard waved him in: "I basically spent the next two months at Universal Studios ... that was how I became an unofficial apprentice that summer."[^cite_note-LongBeach-57][^cite_note-58] His family later moved to [Saratoga, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saratoga,_California), where he attended [Saratoga High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saratoga_High_School_\(California\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997115-59] A year later, his parents divorced. Spielberg moved to Los Angeles to stay with his father,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200424-60] while his three sisters and mother remained in Saratoga. He recalls his mother had "a huge adventurous personality. We always saw her as [Peter Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Pan_\(character\)), the kid who never wanted to grow up, and she sort of saw herself that way. I think my mom lived a lot of childhoods in her ninety-seven years."[^cite_note-FreshAir-20] He was not interested in academics, aspiring only to be a filmmaker.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200422-61] He applied to the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_California)'s film school but was turned down because of his mediocre grades.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997131-62] He then applied and enrolled at [California State University, Long Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_University,_Long_Beach), where he became a brother of [Theta Chi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theta_Chi) fraternity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997141-63] In 1968, Universal gave Spielberg the opportunity to write and direct a short film for theatrical release, the 26-minute [35 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35_mm_movie_film) *[Amblin'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amblin')*. Studio vice president [Sidney Sheinberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Sheinberg) was impressed and offered Spielberg a seven-year directing contract.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200426-64] A year later, he dropped out of college to begin directing television productions for Universal,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997169-65] making him the youngest director to be signed to a long-term plan with a major Hollywood studio.[^cite_note-66]

He recalls a formative encounter with one of his favorite filmmakers, [John Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ford), who said: "So they tell me you want to be a picture maker. You see those paintings around the office?" Spielberg said he did. Ford pointed to a painting and asked, "Where's the horizon?" Spielberg said it was at the top. Ford asked him where it was in another painting. Spielberg said it was at the bottom. Ford said, "When you're able to distinguish the art of the horizon at the bottom of a frame or at the top of the frame, but not going right through the center of the frame, when you can appreciate why it's at the top and why it's at the bottom, you might make a pretty good picture maker."[^cite_note-67]

## Career

### 1969–1974: On the horizon

Spielberg's professional debut was "Eyes" (1969), a *[Night Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_Gallery_\(film\))* segment scripted by [Rod Serling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Serling) and starring [Joan Crawford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Crawford).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997170-68] Initially, there was skepticism from Crawford and studio executives regarding Spielberg's inexperience. His initial contributions received mixed responses, leading Spielberg to briefly step back from studio work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200431-69] Crawford, reflecting on her collaboration with Spielberg, recognized his potential, noting his unique intuitive inspiration. She expressed her appreciation for Spielberg's talent in a note to him and communicated her approval to Serling. Crawford's endorsement highlighted Spielberg's early recognition in Hollywood despite hesitations regarding his experience.[^cite_note-70] He was hired to do the segment "Make Me Laugh". He recalled "I came on the set, and because it was only 11 minutes long I had this idea of shooting the whole 11 minutes in one shot. \[...] But I staged the whole thing across four sets in one shot. Storyboarded it, rehearsed it, everybody was excited about it, and then when the executives sat down to watch the rushes the next day they saw the entire 10-minute take of most of the show. And they were appalled."[^cite_note-71]

In the early 1970s, Spielberg unsuccessfully tried to raise financing for his own low-budget films. He co-wrote and directed teleplays for *[Marcus Welby, M.D.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Welby,_M.D.)*, *[The Name of the Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Name_of_the_Game_\(TV_series\))*, *[Columbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbo), [Owen Marshall, Counselor at Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Marshall,_Counselor_at_Law)* and *[The Psychiatrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Psychiatrist_\(TV_series\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200433-72]* The *Columbo* episode directed was the show's inaugural, non-pilot episode, "[Murder by the Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbo_season_1#Episodes)".[^cite_note-73] Although unsatisfied with his work,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxter199669-74] Spielberg used the opportunity to hone his craft. He earned good reviews and impressed producers; he was earning a steady income and relocated to [Laurel Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurel_Canyon), LA.*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200433-72]*

Spielberg's directorial debut was *[Duel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duel_\(1971_film\))* (1971), adapted from [Richard Matheson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Matheson)'s short story about a salesman ([Dennis Weaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Weaver)) being chased down a highway by a psychotic [tanker truck](2863776.md) driver.[^cite_note-75] Executives decided to promote the film on television. Reviews were positive, and Universal asked Spielberg to shoot more scenes so it could be released theatrically.*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200435-76]* "Deservedly so" writes [David Thomson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Thomson_\(film_critic\)), "for it stands up as one of the medium's most compelling spirals of suspense. The ordinariness of the Dennis Weaver character and the monstrous malignance of the truck confront one another with a narrative assurance that never needs to remind us of the element of [fable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fable)."[^cite_note-Thomson-13] Spielberg described it as "Godzilla vs. Bambi".[^cite_note-77] *Duel* aired on [Barry Diller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Diller)'s [ABC Movie of the Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_Movie_of_the_Week) before its theatrical release.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] More TV films followed: *[Something Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Something_Evil)* (1972), which aired on CBS,[^cite_note-80] and *[Savage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savage_\(1973_TV_film\))* (1973), which aired on NBC.[^cite_note-81]

Spielberg made his theatrical debut with *[The Sugarland Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sugarland_Express)* (1974), based on a true story of a married couple ([Goldie Hawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldie_Hawn) and [William Atherton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Atherton)) on the run, desperate to regain custody of their baby from foster parents.*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200437-82]* The film marked the first of many collaborations with composer [John Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Williams).[^cite_note-King2023-83] It was awarded Best Screenplay at the [1974 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Cannes_Film_Festival)[^cite_note-84] but was not a commercial success,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200438-85] which Spielberg blamed on Universal's inconsistent marketing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxter1996115-86] [Pauline Kael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_Kael) wrote "Spielberg uses his gifts in a very free-and-easy, American way—for humor, and for a physical response to action. He could be that rarity among directors, a born entertainer—perhaps a new generation's [Howard Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Hawks)."[^cite_note-87] *[The Hollywood Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hollywood_Reporter)* wrote that "a major new director is on the horizon".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997223-88]

### 1975–1980: Magician



Poster for *[Jaws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaws_\(film\))* (1975)

Producers [Richard D. Zanuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_D._Zanuck) and [David Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brown_\(producer\)) took a chance by giving Spielberg the opportunity to direct [*Jaws*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaws_\(film\)) (1975), a [thriller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thriller_film) based on [Peter Benchley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Benchley)'s [bestseller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaws_\(novel\)). In it, a [great white shark](43619.md) attacks beachgoers at a [resort town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resort_town), prompting police chief Martin Brody ([Roy Scheider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Scheider)) to hunt it with the help of a [marine biologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_biologist) ([Richard Dreyfuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Dreyfuss)) and a veteran shark hunter ([Robert Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Shaw)). *Jaws* was the first film shot on open ocean,[^cite_note-89] so shooting proved difficult, especially when the mechanical shark malfunctioned. The shooting schedule overran by a hundred days, and Universal threatened to cancel production.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200722-90] Against expectations, *Jaws* was a success, setting the domestic box-office record and making Spielberg a household name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997250-91] It won [Academy Awards](324.md) for [Best Film Editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Film_Editing) ([Verna Fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verna_Fields)), [Academy Award for Best Original Score](83065.md) (John Williams) and [Best Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Sound_Mixing) ([Robert Hoyt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hoyt_\(sound_engineer\)), [Roger Heman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Heman_Jr), [Earl Madery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Madery) and [John Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Carter_\(sound_engineer\))).[^cite_note-92] Spielberg said the malfunctioning mechanical shark resulted in a better movie, as he had to find other ways to suggest the villain's presence. Kael called it the "most cheerfully perverse scare movie ever made", adding "it’s funny in a [Woody Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woody_Allen) way." She quoted Alfred Hitchcock as saying Spielberg thought outside the visual dynamics of the theater: "he’s the first one of us who doesn’t think in terms of the [proscenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proscenium) arch. With him, there’s nothing but the camera lens."[^cite_note-93]

> Like [Coppola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola) on *[The Godfather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Godfather)*, Spielberg asserted his own role and deftly organized the elements into a roller coaster entertainment without sacrificing inner meanings. The suspense of the picture came from meticulous technique and good humor about its own surgical cutting. You have only to submit to the travesty of *[Jaws 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaws_2)* to realize how much more engagingly Spielberg saw the ocean, the perils, the sinister beauty of the shark, and the vitality of its human opponents.

— Critic [David Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Thomson_\(film_critic\))

[^cite_note-Thomson-13]

After declining an offer to make *Jaws 2*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxter1996145-94] Spielberg and Dreyfuss reunited to work on a film about [UFOs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UFOs), *[Close Encounters of the Third Kind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Close_Encounters_of_the_Third_Kind)* (1977). Spielberg used [65 mm film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65_mm_film) for the best picture quality, and a new live-action recording system so recordings could be duplicated later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200726-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997277-96] He cast one of his favorite directors, [François Truffaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Truffaut), as the scientist Claude Lacombe and worked with special effects expert [Douglas Trumbull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Trumbull). It marked the first of many collaborations between Spielberg and editor [Michael Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Kahn_\(film_editor\)).[^cite_note-97] One of the rare films both written and directed by Spielberg, *Close Encounters* won Academy Awards for [Best Cinematography](61834.md) ([Vilmos Zsigmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilmos_Zsigmond)) and [Best Sound Effects Editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_Sound_Effects_Editing) ([Frank Warner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Warner_\(sound_editor\))).[^cite_note-98] [Stanley Kauffmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Kauffmann) wrote: "I saw *Close Encounters* at its first public showing in New York, and most of the audience stayed...to watch the credits crawl lengthily at the end. For one thing, under the credits the giant spaceship was returning to the stars. For another, they just didn't want to leave this picture. For still another, they seemed to understand the importance of those many names to what they had just seen." Kauffmann placed it first on his list of the best American films from 1968 to 1977.[^cite_note-99] Kael called Spielberg "a magician in the age of movies".[^cite_note-100]

His next directorial work was [*1941*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1941_\(film\)) (1979), an action-comedy written by [Robert Zemeckis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Zemeckis) and [Bob Gale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Gale) about Californians preparing for a Japanese invasion after the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor). Spielberg was self-conscious about doing comedy as he had no prior experience in it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200730-101] Universal and Columbia agreed to co-finance the film. *1941* grossed more than \$92 million worldwide upon release,[^cite_note-102] but most critics, and the studio heads, disliked it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200730-101] [Charles Champlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Champlin) described *1941* as "the most conspicuous waste since the last major oil spill, which it somewhat resembles."[^cite_note-103]

### 1981–1992: Impresario

Spielberg directed *[Raiders of the Lost Ark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raiders_of_the_Lost_Ark)* (1981), with a screenplay by [Lawrence Kasdan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Kasdan) based on a story by [George Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Lucas) and [Philip Kaufman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Kaufman). They considered it a homage to the [serials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serial_film) of the 1930s and 40s.*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997312-104]* It starred [Harrison Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrison_Ford) as [Indiana Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones_\(character\)) and [Karen Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Allen) as [Marion Ravenwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_Ravenwood). Filmed in [La Rochelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Rochelle), [Hawaii](13270.md), [Tunisia](30188.md) and [Elstree Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elstree_Studios), England, the shoot was difficult but Spielberg said it helped him hone his business acumen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200732-105] The film was a box-office success[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200733-106] and won Academy Awards for [Best Art Direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_Art_Direction) ([Norman Reynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Reynolds), [Leslie Dilley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leslie_Dilley) and [Michael D. Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_D._Ford)); Best Film Editing ([Michael Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Kahn_\(film_editor\))); [Best Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Sound) ([Bill Varney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Varney), [Steve Maslow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Maslow), [Gregg Landaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregg_Landaker) and [Roy Charman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Charman)); [Best Sound Editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Sound_Editing) ([Ben Burtt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Burtt) and [Richard L. Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_L._Anderson_\(sound_effects_editor\))) and [Best Visual Effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Visual_Effects) ([Richard Edlund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Edlund), [Kit West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kit_West), [Bruce Nicholson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Nicholson) and [Joe Johnston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Johnston)).[^cite_note-107] [Roger Ebert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Ebert) wrote: "*Raiders of the Lost Ark* is an out-of-body experience, a movie of glorious imagination and breakneck speed that grabs you in the first shot, hurtles you through a series of incredible adventures, and deposits you back in reality two hours later–breathless, dizzy, wrung-out, and with a silly grin on your face".[^cite_note-108] *Raiders* was the first film in the *[Indiana Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones)* franchise.



President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) and First Lady [Nancy Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Reagan) with Spielberg at [The White House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_White_House) during a private dinner and showing of *E.T.* in 1982

Spielberg returned to science fiction with *[E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.T._the_Extra-Terrestrial)* (1982). It tells the story of Elliot ([Henry Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Thomas)), a young boy who befriends an alien who was left behind by his companions and is trying to return home. Spielberg eschewed [storyboards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storyboards) so his direction would be more spontaneous and shot roughly in sequence so the actors' performances would be authentic as they bonded with [E.T.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.T._\(character\)) [Richard Corliss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Corliss) wrote, "This was the closing-night attraction at the [1982 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Cannes_Film_Festival), a venue not known for blubbering sentiment. At the end, as the little critter bade his farewells and the [Jules Verne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Verne)-like space ship left the ground, the audience similarly levitated. One heard the audience's childlike applause; one felt their spirits lift. This was rapture made audible, palpable ... Spielberg orchestrated the movements of the camera and the puppet spaceman with the feelings of—it has to be called love—expressed in young Henry Thomas' yearning face. E.T. was the first film character to be a finalist in [TIME's Man of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Person_of_the_Year) sweepstakes. It would have been fine with me if the little creature, this lovely film, had won."[^cite_note-109]

A special screening was organized for [Ronald](25433.md) and [Nancy Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Reagan), who were emotional by the end.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200736-110] *E.T.* grossed \$700 million worldwide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200736-110] It won four Academy Awards: Best Original Score (John Williams), Best Sound ([Robert Knudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Knudson), [Robert Glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Glass_\(sound_engineer\)), [Don Digirolamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Digirolamo) and [Gene Cantamessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Cantamessa)), Best Sound Editing ([Charles L. Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_L._Campbell) and Ben Burtt) and Best Visual Effects ([Carlo Rambaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlo_Rambaldi), [Dennis Muren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Muren) and [Kenneth F. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_F._Smith)).[^cite_note-111] Kael wrote of E.T., "His voice is ancient and otherworldly but friendly, humorous. And this scaly, wrinkled little man with huge, wide-apart, soulful eyes and a jack-in-the-box neck has been so fully created that he's a friend to us, too; when he speaks of his longing to go home the audience becomes as mournful as Elliot. Spielberg has earned the tears that some people in the audience—and not just children—shed. Genuinely entrancing movies are almost as rare as extraterrestrial visitors."[^cite_note-112] Spielberg co-wrote and produced [*Poltergeist*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poltergeist_\(1982_film\)) ([Tobe Hooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobe_Hooper), 1982), released the same summer as *E.T.*[^cite_note-Magic-38] With [John Landis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Landis), he produced the [anthology film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthology_film) *[Twilight Zone: The Movie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twilight_Zone:_The_Movie)* (1983), contributing the "Kick the Can" segment.[^cite_note-Thomson-13]



Spielberg and [Chandran Rutnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandran_Rutnam) in Sri Lanka during the filming of *Indiana Jones and the Temple of Doom*

His next film was the *Raiders* [prequel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prequel) *[Indiana Jones and the Temple of Doom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones_and_the_Temple_of_Doom)* (1984), shot in the US, Sri Lanka and China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200742-113] It was darker than its predecessor, and led to the creation of the [PG-13 rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_Picture_Association_film_rating_system) because some content was deemed unsuitable for children under 13.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997357-114] Spielberg said he was unhappy with *Temple of Doom* because it lacked his "personal touches and love".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200743-115] Nonetheless, the film was a blockbuster hit,[^cite_note-116] won the Academy Award for Best Visual Effects[^cite_note-117] and got good reviews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200743-115] Kael preferred it to the original, writing, "Spielberg is like a magician whose tricks are so daring they make you laugh. He creates an atmosphere of happy disbelief: the more breathtaking and exhilarating the stunts are the funnier they are. Nobody has ever fused thrills and laughter in quite the way that he does here. He starts off at full charge in the opening sequence and just keeps going".[^cite_note-118] Ebert wrote "*Indiana Jones and the Temple of Doom* makes no apologies for being exactly what it is: Exhilarating, manic, wildly imaginative escapism. No apologies are necessary. This is the most cheerfully exciting, bizarre, goofy, romantic adventure movie since *Raiders*, and it is high praise to say that it’s not so much a sequel as an equal."[^cite_note-119] On the project, Spielberg met his future wife, [Kate Capshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Capshaw).[^cite_note-120] Spielberg recalled, "It was a good learning exercise for me to really throw myself into a black hole. I came out of the darkness of *Temple Of Doom* and I entered the light of the woman I was eventually going to marry and raise a family with."[^cite_note-121]

Thomson writes that "At first sight, the Spielberg of the eighties may seem more an impresario—or a studio, even—than a director."[^cite_note-Thomson-13] Between 1984 and 1990, Spielberg served as producer or executive producer on nineteen films. In the early 1980s, Spielberg befriended [Warner Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WarnerMedia#Warner_Communications_\(1972%E2%80%931990\)) CEO [Steve Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Ross_\(businessman\)), leading Spielberg to make films for Warner Bros.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997383-122] The first was [*The Color Purple*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Color_Purple_\(1985_film\)) (1985), an adaptation of [Alice Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Walker)'s [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Color_Purple) about a generation of empowered African-American women in the Depression-era South. It was Spielberg's first film on a dramatic subject, and he expressed reservations about tackling the project: "It's the risk of being judged-and accused of not having the sensibility to do character studies."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200745-123] Starring [Whoopi Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whoopi_Goldberg) and [Oprah Winfrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oprah_Winfrey), the film was a box-office hit and critics started to take note of Spielberg's foray into drama.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200745-123] Ebert named it the best film of the year.[^cite_note-124] The film received eleven Academy Award nominations[^cite_note-125] and Spielberg won [Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directors_Guild_of_America_Award) from the [Directors Guild of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directors_Guild_of_America).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200745-123]

As China underwent [economic reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) and opened up to the American film industry, Spielberg made *[Empire of the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_the_Sun_\(film\))* (1987), the first American film shot in [Shanghai](27643.md) since the 1930s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997395-126] It is an adaptation of [J. G. Ballard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._G._Ballard)'s autobiographical [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_the_Sun_\(novel\)) about Jamie Graham ([Christian Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Bale)), a boy who goes from being the son of a wealthy British family in Shanghai to a [prisoner of war](25008.md) in a Japanese [internment camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_camp) during World War II. David Lean was originally set to direct, with Spielberg producing. It was written by [Tom Stoppard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Stoppard) and co-starred [John Malkovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Malkovich) as an American expatriate. Critical reaction was mixed; criticism ranged from the "overwrought" plot to Spielberg's downplaying of "disease and starvation".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997397-127][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997398-128] However, [Andrew Sarris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Sarris) named it the best film of the year and one of the best of the decade.[^cite_note-129] He wrote: "The film is a fusion of kinesthetic energy with literary sensibility, pulse-pounding adventure with exquisitely delicate sensitivity.”[^cite_note-130] Corliss wrote that "Spielberg has energized each frame with allusive legerdemain and an intelligent density of images and emotions."[^cite_note-131] It was a disappointment at the box office, possibly because it was released around the same time as [John Boorman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Boorman)'s similarly themed *[Hope and Glory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_and_Glory_\(film\))*. The film was nominated for six Academy Awards,[^cite_note-132]; Ian Alterman, in *[The New York Times](30680.md)* thought it had been overlooked.[^cite_note-133] Spielberg recalled that *Empire of the Sun* was one of his most enjoyable films to make.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200747-134] Thomson called it "a great work through and through" and "the first clear sign that Spielberg the showman was an artist, too."[^cite_note-135]



Spielberg, March 1990

Spielberg intended to direct *[Rain Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_Man)* but instead made *[Indiana Jones and the Last Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones_and_the_Last_Crusade)* (1989) to meet his contractual obligations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997400-136] A longtime [James Bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bond) fan, Spielberg cast [Sean Connery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Connery) as Jones's father, [Henry Jones, Sr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Jones,_Sr).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200747-134] Due to complaints about violence in *Temple of Doom*, Spielberg returned to more family-friendly fare for the third installment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200748-137] *Last Crusade* received positive reviews and was a box-office success, earning \$474 million; it was his biggest hit since *E.T.*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997403-138] [Joseph McBride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_McBride_\(writer\)) called it a comeback for Spielberg, and Spielberg acknowledged how much he has learned from the *Indiana Jones* films.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997403-138] Ebert wrote that "*Raiders of the Lost Ark*, now more than ever, seems a turning point in the cinema of escapist entertainment, and there was really no way Spielberg could make it new all over again. What he has done is to take many of the same elements, and apply all of his craft and sense of fun to make them work yet once again. And they do."[^cite_note-139]

That year, he reunited with Richard Dreyfuss for the romantic drama *[Always](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Always_\(1989_film\))*, about an [aerial firefighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_firefighter). It is a modern remake of one of Spielberg's favorite childhood films, *[A Guy Named Joe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Guy_Named_Joe)* (1943). He said, "As a child I was very frustrated, and maybe I saw my own parents \[in *A Guy Named Joe*]. I was also short of girlfriends. And it stuck with me."*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997406-140]* Spielberg had discussed the film with Dreyfuss in 1975, and up to twelve drafts were written before filming commenced.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200748-137] It costarred [Holly Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holly_Hunter) and [John Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Goodman) and was the final film role for [Audrey Hepburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audrey_Hepburn).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200748-137] [Janet Maslin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Maslin) wrote that while "*Always* is filled with big, sentimental moments, it lacks the intimacy to make any of this very moving."[^cite_note-141]

After a brief setback in which he felt "artistically stalled",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200751-142] Spielberg returned with *[Hook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hook_\(film\))* (1991), about a middle-aged [Peter Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Pan_\(character\)) ([Robin Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Williams)), who returns to [Neverland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neverland) and encounters [Tinker Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinker_Bell) ([Julia Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_Roberts)) and the eponymous [Captain Hook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_Hook) ([Dustin Hoffman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dustin_Hoffman)). The stars clashed on set, and Spielberg told *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)* that he would never work with Roberts again.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200752-143] The studio enjoyed the film but most critics did not; [Desson Howe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desson_Howe) described it as "too industrially organized".[^cite_note-144] At the box office, it earned more than \$300 million worldwide from a \$70 million budget.[^cite_note-145]

### 1993–1998: Oscar winner



Spielberg receiving the [Golden Lion](2386128.md) by Italian filmmaker [Gillo Pontecorvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gillo_Pontecorvo) at the [50th Venice International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50th_Venice_International_Film_Festival), 1993

In 1993, Spielberg returned with *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park)*, based on [Michael Crichton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Crichton)'s [bestseller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(novel\)), with a screenplay by Crichton and [David Koepp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Koepp). *Jurassic Park* follows John Hammond ([Richard Attenborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Attenborough)), who has hired geneticists to create a [wildlife park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_theme_park) of [de-extinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-extinct) dinosaurs. It features [Sam Neill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Neill) and [Laura Dern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Dern) as paleontologists and [Jeff Goldblum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Goldblum) as mathematiican Ian Malcolm. In a departure from his usual order of planning, Spielberg and the designers [storyboarded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storyboard) certain sequences from the novel early on.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997419-146] The film combined practical effects by [Stan Winston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Winston) and [computer-generated imagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer-generated_imagery) provided by [Industrial Light & Magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Light_&_Magic); *Jurassic Park* eclipsed *E.T.* as the highest-grossing film to date, and won three Academy Awards.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200754-147][^cite_note-A-148]

That year, Spielberg directed *[Schindler's List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schindler's_List)*, about [Oskar Schindler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oskar_Schindler), a businessman who helped save 1,100 Jews from [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-149] Spielberg waited ten years to make the film as he did not feel "mature" enough.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPogrebin200532-150] He wanted to embrace his heritage,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200755-151][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199718-152] and after the birth of his son, Max, he said that "it greatly affected me \[...] A spirit began to ignite in me, and I became a Jewish dad".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride199721-153] Principal photography began on March 1, 1993, in [Kraków](16815.md), Poland, while Spielberg supervised the post-production of *Jurassic Park*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200756-154] To make filming "bearable", Spielberg brought his wife and children with him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997415-155] *Schindler's List* won seven Academy Awards, including [Best Picture](61702.md) and Spielberg's first as [Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director).[^cite_note-A-148][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200758-156] It also won seven [BAFTAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAFTAs), and three [Golden Globes](142724.md).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158]

Ebert wrote, "[Flaubert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flaubert) once wrote that he disliked *[Uncle Tom's Cabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncle_Tom's_Cabin)* because the author was constantly preaching against slavery. 'Does one have to make observations about slavery?' he asked. 'Depict it; that's enough.' And then he added, 'An author in his book must be like God in the universe, present everywhere and visible nowhere.' That would describe Spielberg, the author of this film. He depicts the evil of the Holocaust, and he tells an incredible story of how it was robbed of some of its intended victims. He does so without the tricks of his trade, the directorial and dramatic contrivances that would inspire the usual melodramatic payoffs. Spielberg is not visible in this film. But his restraint and passion are present in every shot."[^cite_note-159] Filmmaker [Claude Lanzmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Lanzmann) criticized the film for its weak representation of the Holocaust.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997434-160] [Imre Kertész](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imre_Kert%C3%A9sz), a Hungarian author and [concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_concentration_camps) survivor, disliked the film, saying, "I regard as [kitsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitsch) any representation of the Holocaust that is incapable of understanding or unwilling to understand the organic connection between our own deformed mode of life and the very possibility of the Holocaust."[^cite_note-161] Thomson calls it "the most moving film I have ever seen."[^cite_note-Thomson-13]

Spielberg used his percentage of profits to start the [Shoah Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoah_Foundation), a non-profit organization that archives [testimonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testimony) of Holocaust survivors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200759-162]In response to meeting Holocaust survivors during the filming of *Schindler's List,* Spielberg decided to use some of the proceeds from the film to establish the Survivors of the Shoah Visual History Foundation in 1994. The goal was to record the testimonies of survivors "before it was too late". Over 50,000 survivors were recorded. In 2006 the foundation transferred its archives to the University of Southern California and the name was changed to the Visual History Archive (VHA) at the [USC Shoah Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USC_Shoah_Foundation).[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

In 1994, Spielberg took a hiatus to spend more time with his family, and set up his new film studio, [DreamWorks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DreamWorks_Pictures), with [Jeffrey Katzenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Katzenberg) and [David Geffen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Geffen).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcBride1997442-168][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200759-162] He returned with *[The Lost World: Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lost_World:_Jurassic_Park)* (1997). A loose adaptation of Michael Crichton's [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lost_World_\(Crichton_novel\)), it follows Ian Malcolm and his researchers who study dinosaurs at Jurassic Park and are confronted by another team with a different agenda. Spielberg wanted the onscreen creatures to be more realistic than in the first film.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200766-169] Budgeted at \$73 million,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200767-170] *The Lost World: Jurassic Park* opened in May 1997 and was one of the highest grossing [films of the year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_in_film).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreer2001247-171] [J. Hoberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Hoberman) called it "better crafted but less fun" than the first film, while *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* wrote "It looks like a director on autopilot \[...] The special effects brook no argument."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreer2001247-171]



Spielberg speaking at the Pentagon on August 11, 1999, after receiving the Department of Defense Medal for Distinguished Public Service

*[Amistad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amistad_\(film\))* (1997), his first film released under DreamWorks, was based on the true story of the events in 1839 aboard the slave ship *[La Amistad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Amistad)*. Producer [Debbie Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Allen), who had read the book *Amistad I* in 1978, thought Spielberg would be perfect to direct.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200768-172] Spielberg was hesitant taking on the project, afraid it would be compared to *Schindler's List*, but said, "I've never planned my career \[...] In the end I do what I think I gotta do."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200768-172] Starring [Morgan Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Freeman), [Anthony Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Hopkins), [Djimon Hounsou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djimon_Hounsou) and [Matthew McConaughey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_McConaughey), Spielberg used Allen's ten years worth of research to reenact the difficult historical scenes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200767-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreer2001252-173] The film struggled to find an audience, and underperformed at the box office;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200769-174] Spielberg admitted that *Amistad* "became too much of a history lesson".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreer2001258-175]

*[Saving Private Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saving_Private_Ryan)* (1998) follows a group of US soldiers led by Captain Miller ([Tom Hanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Hanks)) sent to bring home a [paratrooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paratrooper) whose three brothers were killed in the same twenty-four hours of the [Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings) landing. Filming took place in England, and [US Marine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Marine) [Dale Dye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Dye) was hired to train the actors and keep them in character during combat scenes. Halfway through filming, Spielberg reminded the cast they were making a tribute to thank "your grandparents and my dad, who fought in \[the war]".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200772-176] Critics praised the direction and its realistic portrayal of war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreer2001274-177] The film grossed a successful \$481 million worldwide[^cite_note-178] and Spielberg won a second Academy Award for Best Director.[^cite_note-The_71st_Academy_Awards_1999-179] In August 1999, Spielberg and Hanks were awarded the [Distinguished Public Service Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_Distinguished_Public_Service_Medal) from [Secretary of Defense](43998.md) [William S. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_S._Cohen).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200772-176][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHorn200239-180] Thomson writes "*Ryan* changed war films: combat, shock, wounds, and fear had never been so graphically presented; and yet there was also a true sense of what duties and ideas had felt like in 1944. I disliked the framing device. I would have admired a director who trusted us to get there without that. Never mind—*Ryan* is a magnificent film."[^cite_note-Thomson-13] Ebert wrote "Spielberg knows how to make audiences weep better than any director since [Chaplin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Chaplin) in *[City Lights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Lights).* But weeping is an incomplete response, letting the audience off the hook. This film embodies ideas. After the immediate experience begins to fade, the implications remain and grow."[^cite_note-181]

### 1999–2012: Master of technology

Spielberg returned to science fiction with *[A.I. Artificial Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.I._Artificial_Intelligence)* (2001), a loose adaptation of [Brian Aldiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Aldiss)'s short story "[Supertoys Last All Summer Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supertoys_Last_All_Summer_Long)" (1969). [Stanley Kubrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Kubrick) had bought the rights to the story in 1979 and worked on an adaptation for years.[^cite_note-182] He told Spielberg about the project in 1984 and suggested that he direct, believing the story was closer to Spielberg's sensibilities. Following Kubrick's death in 1999, Spielberg decided to direct *A.I.*[^cite_note-183] Spielberg tried to be faithful to Kubrick's vision[^cite_note-184] and made allusions to his friend's work.[^cite_note-185] The plot revolves around an [android](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Android_\(robot\)), David ([Haley Joel Osment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haley_Joel_Osment)) who, like [Pinocchio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinocchio), dreams of being a "real boy". The film won five [Saturn Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_Awards)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200775-186] and grossed \$236 million worldwide.[^cite_note-187] [Jonathan Rosenbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Rosenbaum) highly praised the film: "If *A.I. Artificial Intelligence* — a film whose split personality is apparent even in its two-part title — is as much a Kubrick movie as a Spielberg one, this is in large part because it defamiliarizes Spielberg, makes him strange. Yet it also defamiliarizes Kubrick, with equally ambiguous results — making his unfamiliarity familiar. Both filmmakers should be credited for the results—Kubrick for proposing that Spielberg direct the project and Spielberg for doing his utmost to respect Kubrick's intentions while making it a profoundly personal work."[^cite_note-188] [A. O. Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._O._Scott) called it "the best fairy tale–the most disturbing, complex and intellectually challenging boy's adventure story–Mr. Spielberg has made" and chose it as the best film of the year[^cite_note-189] and one of the best of the decade.[^cite_note-190]

Spielberg followed *A.I.* with *[Minority Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_Report)* (2002), based on [Philip K. Dick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_K._Dick)'s 1956 [short story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Minority_Report). The film stars [Tom Cruise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Cruise) as commanding officer of [precrime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precrime) in a futuristic [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) Ebert named *Minority Report* the best film of 2002, praising its craftsmanship: "here is Spielberg using every trick in the book and matching them without seams, so that no matter how he's achieving his effects, the focus is always on the story and the characters ... Some directors place their trust in technology. Spielberg, who is a master of technology, trusts only story and character, and then uses everything else as a workman uses his tools."[^cite_note-191] The film earned more than \$358 million worldwide.[^cite_note-192] Also in 2002, he released *[Catch Me If You Can](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch_Me_If_You_Can)*, based on the [autobiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch_Me_If_You_Can_\(book\)) of con-artist [Frank Abagnale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Abagnale). [Leonardo DiCaprio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_DiCaprio) played Abagnale; [Christopher Walken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Walken) and Hanks also starred. Spielberg said, "I have always loved movies about sensational rogues—they break the law, but you just have to love them for the moxie."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200780-193] The film was a critical and commercial success.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200782-194] That year, he returned to Long Beach, presenting *Schindler's List* to complete his [Bachelor of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts) in Film and Electronic Media.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParish200428-195]



Spielberg with director and friend [George Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Lucas) in 2006

Spielberg followed *Catch Me If You Can* with *[The Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Terminal)* (2004), a comedy inspired by the true story of [Mehran Karimi Nasseri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehran_Karimi_Nasseri)[^cite_note-196] and by [Jacques Tati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Tati)'s *[Playtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playtime)* (1967).[^cite_note-\:BFI-52] The film follows Viktor Navorski (Hanks), an Eastern European man who, after a coup in his home country, is stranded in [John F. Kennedy International Airport](97218.md). It features [Catherine Zeta-Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Zeta-Jones) as a flight attendant and [Stanley Tucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Tucci) as a customs and immigration official. Ebert wrote of Viktor's predicament: "The immigration service, and indeed the American legal system, has no way of dealing with him because Viktor does not do, or fail to do, any of the things the system is set up to prevent him from doing, or not doing. He has slipped through a perfect logical loophole. *The Terminal* is like a sunny [Kakfa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Kakfa) story, in which it is the citizen who persecutes the bureaucracy." The titular terminal was a real set built by [Alex McDowell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_McDowell).[^cite_note-197] In 2005, Spielberg directed *[War of the Worlds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Worlds_\(2005_film\)),* a co-production of Paramount and DreamWorks, based on [H. G. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._G._Wells)'s [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_War_of_the_Worlds); Spielberg had been a fan of the book and of [George Pal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Pal)'s [1953 film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_War_of_the_Worlds_\(1953_film\)).[^cite_note-198] The film follows an American [dock worker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dock_worker) (Cruise) who is forced to look after his children, from whom he lives separately, as he tries to reunite them with their mother when extraterrestrials invade Earth. Spielberg used storyboards to help the actors react to computer imagery and used natural lighting and camerawork to avoid an "over stylized" science fiction picture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200784-199] The film grossed more than \$600 million worldwide.[^cite_note-200]

Spielberg's *[Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_\(2005_film\))* (2005) is about the Israeli government's secret retaliation after 11 Israeli Olympic athletes were murdered in the 1972 [Munich massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_massacre). The film is based on *[Vengeance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vengeance_\(Jonas_book\))*, a book by journalist [George Jonas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Jonas).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200786-201] It was previously adapted for the screen in the 1986 television film *[Sword of Gideon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sword_of_Gideon)*. Spielberg, who personally remembers the incident, sought advice from former president [Bill Clinton](3356.md), among others, before making the film, because he did not want to cause further problems in the Middle East.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200786-201] Although the film garnered mostly positive reviews, some critics perceived it as antisemitic;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200787-202] it is one of Spielberg's most controversial films.[^cite_note-203] *Munich* received five Academy Awards nominations: Best Picture, Best Film Editing, Best Score, [Best Adapted Screenplay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Adapted_Screenplay) and Best Director.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdge2008102-205]



Spielberg with [Tom Hanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Hanks) promoting *[The Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pacific_\(miniseries\))* in Washington, D.C.

In the mid-2000s, Spielberg scaled down his directing career and became more selective about film projects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201460-206] In December 2005, he and his partners sold DreamWorks to conglomerate [Viacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viacom_\(2005%E2%80%932019\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdge2008102-205] In June 2006, Spielberg planned to make *[Interstellar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_\(film\))*, but abandoned the project, which was eventually directed by [Christopher Nolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Nolan).[^cite_note-207] Spielberg remained active as a producer. Spielberg returned to the *Indiana Jones* series in 2008 with *[Indiana Jones and the Kingdom of the Crystal Skull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Jones_and_the_Kingdom_of_the_Crystal_Skull).* Released 19 years after *Last Crusade*, the film is set in 1957, pitting Indiana Jones against [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) agents led by Irina Spalko ([Cate Blanchett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cate_Blanchett)), searching for a [telepathic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telepath) [crystal skull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_skull). [Principal photography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_photography) was complete in October 2007, and the film was released in May 2008.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] This was his first film not released by DreamWorks since 1997.[^cite_note-210] The film received generally favorable reviews from critics, but some fans were disappointed by the introduction of science fiction elements uncharacteristic of the previous films.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHook201090-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201460-206] Tom Ryan praised Spielberg and Lucas for their realistic 1950s setting.[^cite_note-212] It was a box-office success, grossing \$790 million worldwide.[^cite_note-213]

In 2009, Spielberg started shooting the first film in a planned trilogy of [motion capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_capture) films based on [Hergé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herg%C3%A9)'s *[The Adventures of Tintin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Tintin)*.[^cite_note-214] Spielberg had long been a fan of the comics and, per [Michael Farr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Farr), Hergé "thought Spielberg was the only person who could ever do Tintin justice."[^cite_note-215] *[The Adventures of Tintin: The Secret of the Unicorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Tintin:_The_Secret_of_the_Unicorn)* was co-produced by [Peter Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Jackson) and premiered in [Brussels](3708.md).[^cite_note-216] The film was released in North American theaters on December 21, 2011, in [Digital 3D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_3D) and [IMAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMAX_3D).[^cite_note-reldates-217] It received generally positive reviews from critics and grossed over \$373 million worldwide.[^cite_note-218] *The Adventures of Tintin* won Best Animated Effects and Best Music at the [39th Annie Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/39th_Annie_Awards).[^cite_note-219] Ebert wrote "*Tintin* is an ambitious and lively caper, miles smarter than your average 3-D family film." Paraphrasing Hergé's [Captain Haddock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_Haddock), he added "I give it 875 portholes."[^cite_note-220]

Spielberg followed *Tintin* with *[War Horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Horse_\(film\))* (2011), an adaptation of [Michael Morpurgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Morpurgo)'s [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Horse_\(novel\)). It follows the friendship between a British boy and his horse Joey before and during World War I.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201464-221] Distributed by [Walt Disney Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Studios_Motion_Pictures) with whom DreamWorks made a distribution deal in 2009, *War Horse* was the first of four consecutive Spielberg films released by Disney. It received acclaim from critics[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201464-221] and was nominated for six [Academy Awards](324.md), including Best Picture.[^cite_note-222] Andrew O'Hehir wrote, "at this point in his career Spielberg is pursuing personal goals, and everything that's terrific and overly flat and tooth-rottingly sweet about *War Horse* reflects that."[^cite_note-223]



Spielberg with Bill Clinton, 2009

Spielberg directed the historical drama *[Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_\(film\))* (2012), starring [Daniel Day-Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Day-Lewis) as President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) and [Sally Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sally_Field) as [Mary Todd Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Todd_Lincoln).[^cite_note-224] Based on [Doris Kearns Goodwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doris_Kearns_Goodwin)'s book *[Team of Rivals: The Political Genius of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_of_Rivals:_The_Political_Genius_of_Abraham_Lincoln)* and written by [Tony Kushner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Kushner), the film depicts the final four months of Lincoln's life. The film was shot in [Richmond, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond,_Virginia) in late 2011.[^cite_note-225] and was released in the US in November 2012.[^cite_note-226] *Lincoln* was acclaimed and earned more than \$250 million worldwide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201465-227] It was nominated for twelve Academy Awards, including Best Picture and Best Director,[^cite_note-228] winning [Best Production Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Production_Design) and [Best Actor](23245410.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMara201464-221] Donald Clarke from *[The Irish Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irish_Times)* wrote "Against the odds, Spielberg makes something genuinely exciting of the backstage wheedling."[^cite_note-229]

### 2013–present: Recent work



Spielberg in 2017.

*[Bridge of Spies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridge_of_Spies_\(film\))* (2015) is a [Cold War](325329.md) thriller based on the [1960 U-2 incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_U-2_incident), focusing on [James B. Donovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_B._Donovan)'s negotiations with the Soviets for the release of pilot [Gary Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Powers) after his aircraft was shot down over Soviet territory. It was written by [Matt Charman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Charman) and the [Coen brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coen_brothers) and starred Tom Hanks, [Mark Rylance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Rylance), [Amy Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Ryan) and [Alan Alda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Alda).[^cite_note-June14dates-230] It was filmed in the fall of 2014 in New York City, Berlin and [Wrocław](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw).[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] *Bridge of Spies* was popular with critics[^cite_note-233] and Rylance won [Best Supporting Actor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Supporting_Actor) at the 88th Academy Awards.[^cite_note-234]

*[The BFG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_BFG_\(2016_film\))* (2016) is an adaptation of [Roald Dahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roald_Dahl)'s [children's book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_BFG), starring [Mark Rylance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Rylance) as the titular Big Friendly Giant and newcomer [Ruby Barnhill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Barnhill). DreamWorks bought the rights in 2010, and [John Madden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Madden_\(director\)) had intended to direct.[^cite_note-235] It was the last film written by *E.T.* screenwriter [Melissa Mathison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melissa_Mathison).[^cite_note-236] It was co-produced and released by [Walt Disney Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Pictures), marking the first Disney-branded film directed by Spielberg. *The BFG* premiered out-of-competition at the [2016 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Cannes_Film_Festival),[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] and received a wide release in the US on July 1, 2016.[^cite_note-June14dates-230] *The BFG* received fair reviews; [Michael Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Phillips_\(critic\)) of *[The Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chicago_Tribune)* compared certain scenes to the works of Alfred Hitchcock and Stanley Kubrick,[^cite_note-239] while Liz Braun thought that there were "moments of wonder and delight" but it was too long.[^cite_note-240]

*[The Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Post_\(film\))* (2017) is an account of *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*'s printing of the *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)*.[^cite_note-241] Starring Tom Hanks and [Meryl Streep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meryl_Streep), production began in New York on May 30, 2017.[^cite_note-242] Spielberg stated his attraction to the project: "When I read the first draft of the script, this wasn't something that could wait three years or two years—this was a story I felt we needed to tell today."[^cite_note-243] It received a wide release on January 12, 2018.[^cite_note-244] *The Post* gained positive reception; Lindsey Bahr wrote "Spielberg infuses every scene with tension and life and the grandeur of the ordinary that he's always been so good at conveying."[^cite_note-245] In 2017, Spielberg, [Paul Greengrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Greengrass), [Francis Ford Coppola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola), [Guillermo del Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro) and [Lawrence Kasdan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Kasdan) were featured in the [Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix) documentary series *[Five Came Back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Came_Back_\(TV_series\))*, about the war-related works of directors [Frank Capra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Capra), [John Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ford), [John Huston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Huston), [George Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stevens) and [William Wyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wyler).[^cite_note-246] He executive produced the series with Barry Diller and [Scott Rudin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Rudin).



Spielberg collaborated with playwright [Tony Kushner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Kushner) for *[West Side Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Side_Story_\(2021_film\))* (2021) and *[The Fabelmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fabelmans)* (2022)

Spielberg directed [*Ready Player One*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ready_Player_One_\(film\)) (2018), adapted from [Ernest Cline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Cline)'s [novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ready_Player_One). It stars [Tye Sheridan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tye_Sheridan), [Olivia Cooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivia_Cooke), [Ben Mendelsohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Mendelsohn), [Lena Waithe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lena_Waithe), [T.J. Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T.J._Miller), [Simon Pegg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Pegg) and Mark Rylance. The plot takes place in 2045 when much of humanity uses [virtual reality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtual_reality) to escape the real world. *Ready Player One* began production in July 2016,[^cite_note-247] and was intended to be released on December 15, 2017,[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] but was moved to March 2018 to avoid competition with *[Star Wars: The Last Jedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Wars:_The_Last_Jedi)*.[^cite_note-250] It premiered at the 2018 [South by Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_by_Southwest) film festival.[^cite_note-251] Spielberg's direction was praised along with the action scenes and visual effects, but many critics thought it was too long and overused 1980s nostalgia.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]
